    Wait, is it possible that the fully contained triplets are NOT consecutive?
    Length 9 block.
    Indices $k, \dots, k+8$.
    Triplets are size 3.
    If $T_j$ is fully contained, indices $3j-2, 3j-1, 3j$ are in block.
    If $T_{j+2}$ is fully contained, indices $3j+4, 3j+5, 3j+6$ are in block.
    Gap between $T_j$ end ($3j$) and $T_{j+2}$ start ($3j+4$) is indices $3j+1, 3j+2, 3j+3$.
    These belong to $T_{j+1}$.

    The gap is $3j+1, 3j+2, 3j+3$.

    So $T_{j+1}$ must be fully contained.
    So fully contained triplets must be consecutive.
    So my analysis of consecutive triplets holds.

    So the maximum ratio of sums of consecutive triplets fully contained in a length-9 block is $1.5$.
    Wait, what if $q < 0$?
    The problem says $q$ is a real number.
    If $q$ is negative, $q^3$ is negative.
    But sums of triplets are $2n > 0$.
    Sums of GP blocks must be positive.
    Sum = $A q^j (1+q+q^2)$.
    $1+q+q^2$ is always positive for real $q$ (discriminant $1-4=-3 < 0$).
    So sign of sum depends on sign of $A q^j$.
    For $T_j$ sum $2j > 0$, we need $A q^j > 0$.
    For $T_{j+1}$ sum $2(j+1) > 0$, we need $A q^{j+3} > 0$.
    $A q^{j+3} = (A q^j) q^3$.
    Since both are positive, $q^3$ must be positive.
    So $q > 0$.
    So we only care about positive $q$.

    **Is there any trick?**

    Let's double check the calculation for Case 1.

    Constraints:
    1. $a_1 + a_2 + a_3 = 2 \implies a_1 + y + yq = 2$.
       $a_1 = 2 - y(1+q)$.
       We can choose $y$ such that $a_1$ is real? Yes.
       Wait, is there any constraint on $a_1$?
       $a_1$ is just a number.
       However, $a_1$ is part of the sequence.
       Does $a_1$ affect anything else?
       No, $S_{3n}$ constraints are sums of blocks.
       $a_1$ is only in Block 1 sum.
       So $a_1$ is free (determined by $y$).
    2. $a_4 + a_5 + a_6 = 4$.
       $yq^2 + yq^3 + yq^4 = yq^2(1+q+q^2) = 4$.
       This determines $y$ given $q$.
       $y = \frac{4}{q^2(1+q+q^2)}$.
       Since $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5} > 0$, $y$ is well defined positive real.
    3. $a_7 + a_8 + a_9 = 6$.
       $yq^5(1+q+q^2) = 6$.
       Check consistency: $y q^5 \Phi = (y q^2 \Phi) q^3 = 4 \cdot 1.5 = 6$.
       Consistent.
    4. $a_{10} + a_{11} + a_{12} = 8$.
       $yq^8 + a_{11} + a_{12} = 8$.
       We can choose $a_{11}, a_{12}$ freely.
       E.g., $a_{11} = 8 - yq^8, a_{12} = 0$.
    5. $a_{13} + a_{14} + a_{15} = 10$.
       Terms not in GP. Free.
    So sequence exists.
    So $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$ is achievable.

    **Is there any case yielding higher $q$?**
    We established $q^3 = \frac{S_{next}}{S_{current}}$.

    **Wait, is it possible that the "full" triplets are not consecutive in index?**
    No, we proved that.
    **Is it possible that $q$ is negative?**

    **Is it possible that $a_n$ are complex?**
    "实数q" (real number q), "数列{a_n}" (sequence of numbers). Usually implies real sequence in this context, or at least $a_n$ should be real for the sums to be real (sums are integers). If $a_n$ complex, sums could be real, but usually "sequence" implies real unless specified. Also "real number q".
    Let's assume $a_n$ real.

    **Let's check if there is any other interpretation.**
    "存在数列{a_n}" - there exists a sequence.
    "对于任意n" - for all n.

    "公比为q" - common ratio q.
    Everything seems standard.

    Let's check if I missed any constraints on $y$.
    $y = a_k$.
    $y = \frac{4}{q^2(1+q+q^2)}$.
    We need $y$ to be real.
    $1+q+q^2 > 0$ for real $q$.
    $q^2 > 0$ for $q \ne 0$.
    So $y$ is real.
    Also need $a_1 = 2 - y(1+q)$ to be real. Yes.
    Need $a_{11}, a_{12}$ real. Yes.
    Are there any hidden constraints?

    Maybe $q$ cannot be 1?
    If $q=1$, sums would be constant?

    If $a_n = c$, sum of 3 terms $3c = 2n$.
    $c = 2n/3$. Not constant.
    So $q \ne 1$.
    $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5} \ne 1$.

    **Let's consider if $q$ could be larger.**
    What if the block of 9 terms is $a_k, \dots, a_{k+8}$ but the GP ratio is $q$?
    Wait, could the terms be $a_k, a_{k+1}, \dots$ but the ratio is defined differently?

    Standard definition.

    **Let's double check the Case 1 impossibility.**
    $q^3 = 2$ and $q^3 = 1.5$.

    Maybe $1+q+q^2 = 0$?

    If $y=0$, then all terms 0.
    Sums would be 0.
    But sums are $2, 4, 6 \dots$.
    So $y \ne 0$.
    So Case 1 is strictly impossible.

    **Is it possible to have $k$ such that we get a higher ratio?**
    We found ratio depends on $j$, index of first full triplet.
    Ratio $\frac{j+1}{j}$.
    Decreasing with $j$.
    Min $j=2$.
    Max ratio 1.5.
    Is it possible to have a block covering $T_1$ partially and $T_2$ fully, but the GP alignment gives a different relation?
    Wait, the relation $q^3 = \frac{Sum(T_{next})}{Sum(T_{curr})}$ was derived assuming the GP terms in $T_{next}$ are $q^3$ times the terms in $T_{curr}$.
    Let's verify this scaling.
    In Case 2 ($k=3m-1$):
    $T_{m+1}$ terms: $a_{3m+1}, a_{3m+2}, a_{3m+3}$.
    Indices relative to start $k=3m-1$:
    $a_{3m+1}$ is index $k+2$. Term $u_2 = y q^2$.
    $a_{3m+2}$ is index $k+3$. Term $u_3 = y q^3$.
    $a_{3m+3}$ is index $k+4$. Term $u_4 = y q^4$.
    Sum $S_{m+1} = y q^2 + y q^3 + y q^4 = y q^2(1+q+q^2)$.
    $T_{m+2}$ terms: $a_{3m+4}, a_{3m+5}, a_{3m+6}$.
    Indices relative to start:
    $a_{3m+4}$ is index $k+5$. Term $u_5 = y q^5$.
    $a_{3m+5}$ is index $k+6$. Term $u_6 = y q^6$.
    $a_{3m+6}$ is index $k+7$. Term $u_7 = y q^7$.
    Sum $S_{m+2} = y q^5 + y q^6 + y q^7 = y q^5(1+q+q^2)$.
    Ratio $S_{m+2} / S_{m+1} = q^3$.
    Yes, correct.

    In Case 3 ($k=3m$):
    $T_{m+1}$ terms: $a_{3m+1}, a_{3m+2}, a_{3m+3}$.
    Start $k=3m$.
    $a_{3m+1}$ is index $k+1$. Term $v_1 = v_0 q^1$.
    $a_{3m+2}$ is index $k+2$. Term $v_2 = v_0 q^2$.
    $a_{3m+3}$ is index $k+3$. Term $v_3 = v_0 q^3$.
    Sum $S_{m+1} = v_0 q(1+q+q^2)$.
    $T_{m+2}$ terms: $a_{3m+4}, a_{3m+5}, a_{3m+6}$.
    $a_{3m+4}$ is index $k+4$. Term $v_4 = v_0 q^4$.
    $a_{3m+5}$ is index $k+5$. Term $v_5 = v_0 q^5$.
    $a_{3m+6}$ is index $k+6$. Term $v_6 = v_0 q^6$.
    Sum $S_{m+2} = v_0 q^4(1+q+q^2)$.
    Ratio $S_{m+2} / S_{m+1} = q^3$.
    Yes, correct.

    So the derivation holds.
    The ratio of sums of consecutive fully contained triplets must be $q^3$.
    The sums of triplets are $2, 4, 6, 8, \dots$.
    Consecutive sums ratio is $\frac{2(n+1)}{2n} = \frac{n+1}{n} = 1 + \frac{1}{n}$.
    We need $q^3 = 1 + \frac{1}{n}$.
    To maximize $q$, maximize ratio, minimize $n$.
    $n$ is the index of the first fully contained triplet.
    We established $n \ge 2$.
    Why $n \ge 2$?
    Because if $n=1$, the triplet $T_1$ (indices 1,2,3) is fully contained.
    This implies the block covers $\{1,2,3\}$.
    Block length 9.
    If it covers $\{1,2,3\}$, it must start at $k \le 1$.
    Since $k \ge 1$, $k=1$.
    If $k=1$, block is $1..9$.
    This covers $T_1, T_2, T_3$ fully.
    This requires $q^3 = S_2/S_1 = 2$ AND $q^3 = S_3/S_2 = 1.5$.
    Contradiction.
    So $n$ cannot be 1.
    Thus min $n=2$.
    Max ratio $1 + 1/2 = 1.5$.
    Max $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.

    **Is there any edge case?**
    What if $q < 0$?
    If $q < 0$, $q^3 < 0$.
    Ratio of sums must be positive.
    But $S_{n+1}/S_n > 0$.
    So $q^3 > 0 \implies q > 0$.
    So $q$ must be positive.

    **What if the block doesn't align with triplets such that we have 2 full triplets?**
    We analyzed all alignments.
    $k \equiv 1 \pmod 3$: 3 full triplets. Impossible.
    $k \equiv 2 \pmod 3$: 2 full triplets.
    $k \equiv 0 \pmod 3$: 2 full triplets.
    Are there other alignments?
    No, modulo 3 covers all cases.

    **Is it possible that the GP ratio $q$ is such that terms are 0?**
    If $a_k = 0$, then all terms 0.
    Sums 0. Contradiction.
    So $a_k \ne 0$.
    If $q=0$, terms become 0 after first.
    Sums would be 0 eventually. Contradiction.
    So $q \ne 0$.

    **Calculation of value:**
    $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5} = \sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$.
    Is this the final answer format?
    Usually simplified radical or fraction.
    $\sqrt[3]{\frac{3}{2}}$ or $\frac{\sqrt[3]{12}}{2}$?
    $\sqrt[3]{1.5}$.

    Let's check if there's any constraint I missed.
    "实数q" - Real number.
    "数列" - Sequence.
    "连续9项" - Consecutive 9 terms.
    "公比" - Common ratio.
    Everything seems covered.

    Let's re-read carefully.
    "存在数列{a_n}" - There exists a sequence.
    "使得对于任意n \in N^*，均有a_1 + ... + a_{3n} = n^2 + n" - Sums condition.
    "且{a_n}中有某连续9项...是公比为q的等比数列" - GP condition.
    "q的最大值" - Max value of q.

    Wait, could the 9 terms be $a_k, \dots, a_{k+8}$ where $k$ is very large?
    Yes, but that gives smaller $q$.
    We want max $q$.
    So we look for small indices.
    We found $k=2$ or $k=3$ gives $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.
    $k=1$ impossible.
    Are there any other $k$?
    $k=2 \implies$ block $a_2..a_{10}$.
    Fully contained triplets $T_2, T_3$.
    $T_2$ sum 4. $T_3$ sum 6.
    Ratio 1.5.
    $k=3 \implies$ block $a_3..a_{11}$.
    Fully contained $T_2, T_3$.
    Same ratio.
    $k=4 \implies$ block $a_4..a_{12}$.
    $k \equiv 1 \pmod 3$.
    Case 1. Impossible.
    $k=5 \implies$ block $a_5..a_{13}$.
    $k \equiv 2 \pmod 3$.
    Fully contained triplets?
    Block indices 5..13.
    Triplets:
    $T_1 [1,3]$ - No.
    $T_2 [4,6]$ - Overlap 5,6. Partial.
    $T_3 [7,9]$ - Full. (7,8,9).
    $T_4 [10,12]$ - Full. (10,11,12).
    $T_5 [13,15]$ - Overlap 13. Partial.
    So fully contained are $T_3, T_4$.
    Sums $2(3)=6, 2(4)=8$.
    Ratio $8/6 = 4/3 \approx 1.33$.
    $q^3 = 1.33 \implies q \approx 1.1$.
    Smaller than 1.5.
    Generally, ratio $\frac{n+1}{n}$ decreases as $n$ increases.
    So $k=2$ or $k=3$ gives the max.

    **Is there any possibility of negative $q$ with alternating signs?**
    If $q < 0$, $q^3 < 0$.
    But sums $S_{3n}$ are positive.
    The sums of full triplets are sums of 3 terms.
    In a GP with $q<0$, terms alternate signs.
    $x, -|x|q, xq^2, -|x|q^3 \dots$
    Sum of 3 terms $x(1+q+q^2)$.
    If $q$ is negative, $1+q+q^2$ can be positive or negative?
    Discriminant of $q^2+q+1$ is $-3$.
    So $q^2+q+1 > 0$ for all real $q$.
    So sum sign depends on sign of first term $x$.
    For $T_j$ sum to be positive ($2j$), $x q^{offset}$ must be positive.
    For $T_{j+1}$ sum to be positive ($2(j+1)$), $x q^{offset+3}$ must be positive.
    $x q^{offset+3} = (x q^{offset}) q^3$.
    If $x q^{offset} > 0$, then $q^3$ must be $> 0$.
    So $q > 0$.
    So negative $q$ is impossible.

    **Double check the "Case 1" impossibility.**
    Is it absolutely impossible?
    Maybe $a_1 + a_2 + a_3 = 2$
    $a_4 + a_5 + a_6 = 4$
    $a_7 + a_8 + a_9 = 6$
    GP $a_1, \dots, a_9$.
    $a_1, a_2, a_3$ sum to 2.
    $a_4 = a_1 q^3$, etc.
    Sum 2 = $q^3 \times$ Sum 1.
    $4 = 2 q^3 \implies q^3 = 2$.
    Sum 3 = $q^3 \times$ Sum 2.
    $6 = 4 q^3 \implies q^3 = 1.5$.
    Contradiction $2 = 1.5$.
    Yes, impossible.
    UNLESS $1+q+q^2 = 0$.
    But $q$ is real.
    So impossible.

    Maybe the GP is $a_k, \dots, a_{k+8}$ but the ratio is $q$?
    Yes.
    Maybe the sum condition is $S_{3n}$?
    Yes.
    Maybe $n$ in $S_{3n}$ is not the triplet index?
    $a_1 + \dots + a_{3n}$.
    Yes, sum of first $3n$ terms.
    This is sum of first $n$ triplets.
    So $\sum_{i=1}^n (a_{3i-2}+a_{3i-1}+a_{3i}) = n^2+n$.
    This implies sum of $i$-th triplet is $(n^2+n) - ((n-1)^2+(n-1)) = n^2+n - (n^2-2n+1+n-1) = n^2+n - (n^2-n) = 2n$.
    Yes, derivation correct.

    Seems straightforward. The bottleneck is the conflict between geometric progression sum scaling and arithmetic progression sum scaling of triplets.

    $1+q+q^2$ is real.
    So $A$ must be real (or purely imaginary if product is real? No, $A$ is term $a_k$).
    If $A$ is imaginary, $a_k$ imaginary.
    But $a_k$ is part of sum.
    If $a_k$ is imaginary, sum might be complex.
    But sum is real.
    So imaginary parts must cancel.
    But terms in GP are $A, Aq, Aq^2$.
    If $A$ is pure imaginary $i b$.
    Terms $ib, ibq, ibq^2$.
    Sum $ib(1+q+q^2)$.
    For this to be real, $b(1+q+q^2)$ must be 0?
    Since $1+q+q^2 \ne 0$, $b=0$.
    So $A$ must be real.
    So $a_n$ must be real.
    So standard assumption holds.

    So $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.

    Let's write down the steps clearly.
    1.  Determine triplet sums: $T_n = a_{3n-2} + a_{3n-1} + a_{3n} = 2n$.
    2.  A block of 9 consecutive terms in GP with ratio $q$ spans 3 triplets.
    3.  Due to alignment, the block fully contains either 0, 1, 2, or 3 triplets.
    4.  Analysis shows 3 full triplets leads to contradiction ($q^3=2$ and $q^3=1.5$).
    5.  Alignment with 2 full triplets is possible.
    6.  For 2 full triplets $T_j, T_{j+1}$, we must have $q^3 = T_{j+1}/T_j = \frac{2(j+1)}{2j} = \frac{j+1}{j}$.
    7.  To maximize $q$, maximize ratio $\frac{j+1}{j}$, so minimize $j$.
    8.  Geometric constraints on block length 9 prevent $j=1$ (would require 3 full triplets).
    9.  Min valid $j=2$.
    10. Max ratio $3/2 = 1.5$.
    11. Max $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.

    Is there any case where block contains 1 full triplet?
    We argued that length 9 block must contain at least 2 full triplets.
    Let's verify this rigorously.
    Block $[k, k+8]$. Length 9.
    Triplets $[3i-2, 3i]$. Length 3.
    If block contains 0 full triplets, it must miss at least one term from every triplet it overlaps.
    But block is contiguous.
    If it misses a term from $T_i$, say $x$, and misses a term from $T_{i+1}$, say $y$.
    If $x < y$, and block contains indices between $x$ and $y$?
    Actually, if block covers a range of indices, say $[k, k+8]$.
    It covers a set of integers.
    For a triplet $T_i$ to be NOT fully contained, the block must exclude at least one index from $T_i$.
    Indices of $T_i$ are $3i-2, 3i-1, 3i$.
    If block excludes $3i-2$, it must start after $3i-2$, i.e., $k > 3i-2$.
    If block excludes $3i-1$, $k > 3i-1$ or $k+8 < 3i-1$.
    If block excludes $3i$, $k+8 < 3i$ or $k > 3i$.
    Basically, if block doesn't contain $T_i$ fully, it must "cut" it.
    Since block length is 9, it covers a lot of ground.
    It spans roughly 3 triplets.
    To have 0 full triplets, it would have to cut all 3 triplets it overlaps.
    Overlap with 3 triplets implies it spans at least from start of $T_1$ to end of $T_3$?
    No, could be $T_1, T_2, T_3$ partially.
    E.g. indices $2..10$.
    $T_1$ (1,2,3) -> has 2,3. Misses 1.
    $T_2$ (4,5,6) -> has 4,5,6. Full.
    $T_3$ (7,8,9) -> has 7,8,9. Full.
    $T_4$ (10,11,12) -> has 10. Misses 11,12.
    So 2 full.
    Can we shift to eliminate $T_2$ full?
    Shift right. $3..11$.
    $T_1$ miss 1,2.
    $T_2$ (4,5,6) -> Full.
    $T_3$ (7,8,9) -> Full.
    $T_4$ miss 12.
    Still 2 full.
    Shift right again. $4..12$.
    $T_1$ miss.
    $T_2$ Full.
    $T_3$ Full.
    $T_4$ Full.
    3 full. (Impossible).
    Shift right. $5..13$.
    $T_2$ miss 4.
    $T_3$ Full.
    $T_4$ Full.
    $T_5$ miss 14,15.
    2 full.
    So pattern is 2, 2, 3, 2, 2, 3...
    We can never have 0 or 1 full triplet with length 9 block?
    Let's check length.
    To have 0 full triplets, we need to miss at least 1 term from each triplet in the span.
    Span of 9 terms covers range of size 9.
    Suppose it covers parts of $T_A, T_B, T_C$.
    To miss a term in $T_A$, we must start after first term or end before last term.
    If we miss 1 term in $T_A$, we "lose" 1 index from full count?
    Actually, number of full triplets is related to how much the block aligns.
    Length 9.
    If block is $[x, x+8]$.
    Number of integers in $[x, x+8]$ is 9.
    Number of multiples of 3 in range?
    Full triplet $T_i$ is contained if $[3i-2, 3i] \subseteq [x, x+8]$.
    This requires $x \le 3i-2$ and $3i \le x+8$.
    $3i-2 \ge x \implies 3i \ge x+2$.
    $3i \le x+8$.
    So $x+2 \le 3i \le x+8$.
    We need to find number of integers $i$ satisfying this.
    Length of interval $[x+2, x+8]$ is 6.
    Multiples of 3 in interval of length 6.
    Can be 2 or 3 multiples of 3.
    E.g. if $x=1$, interval $[3, 9]$. Multiples 3, 6, 9. (3 multiples).
    If $x=2$, interval $[4, 10]$. Multiples 6, 9. (2 multiples).
    If $x=3$, interval $[5, 11]$. Multiples 6, 9. (2 multiples).
    If $x=4$, interval $[6, 12]$. Multiples 6, 9, 12. (3 multiples).
    So yes, always 2 or 3 full triplets.
    Since 3 is impossible, valid blocks have exactly 2 full triplets.
    So the derivation holds.

    Final check: $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.

    No, we saw freedom in partial triplets.

    So $q \ne 1$.

    One detail: "连续9项" - consecutive 9 terms.
